Well… mostly I came in because I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to. I work with loans and credit counseling, so I’m used to paperwork and follow-up lists and details, but lately I’ll be sitting there staring at a loan worksheet or a client call list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still haven’t really taken it in.

And then I start putting things off, like making calls or deciding what to handle first, because even simple decisions feel harder than they should. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.

I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.

Some days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.

So I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.

I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.

At work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.

And outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.

have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.

But whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.

So I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to. I work with loans and credit counseling, so I’m used to paperwork and follow-up lists and details, but lately I’ll be sitting there staring at a loan worksheet or a client call list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd then I start putting things off, like making calls or deciding what to handle first, because even simple decisions feel harder than they should. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t always walk around crying or anything like that, so sometimes I almost talk myself out of calling it “sad,” but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to make me feel better — like going over to the community center, seeing people I know, or spending time in my garden — I’m there, and I’m doing the activity, but it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I’ll smile and chat because that’s what you do, you know, but it feels pasted on. Like I’m performing being okay more than actually feeling okay.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. At first I told myself it was just grief, you know, that of course I’d feel off and heavy for a while because she was such a steady person in my life. But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.

But it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.

So… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to. I work with loans and credit counseling, so I’m used to paperwork and follow-up lists and details, but lately I’ll be sitting there staring at a loan worksheet or a client call list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd then I start putting things off, like making calls or deciding what to handle first, because even simple decisions feel harder than they should. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t always walk around crying or anything like that, so sometimes I almost talk myself out of calling it “sad,” but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to make me feel better — like going over to the community center, seeing people I know, or spending time in my garden — I’m there, and I’m doing the activity, but it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I’ll smile and chat because that’s what you do, you know, but it feels pasted on. Like I’m performing being okay more than actually feeling okay.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. At first I told myself it was just grief, you know, that of course I’d feel off and heavy for a while because she was such a steady person in my life. But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.

I guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to. I work with loans and credit counseling, so I’m used to paperwork and follow-up lists and details, but lately I’ll be sitting there staring at a loan worksheet or a client call list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd then I start putting things off, like making calls or deciding what to handle first, because even simple decisions feel harder than they should. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t always walk around crying or anything like that, so sometimes I almost talk myself out of calling it “sad,” but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to make me feel better — like going over to the community center, seeing people I know, or spending time in my garden — I’m there, and I’m doing the activity, but it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I’ll smile and chat because that’s what you do, you know, but it feels pasted on. Like I’m performing being okay more than actually feeling okay.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. At first I told myself it was just grief, you know, that of course I’d feel off and heavy for a while because she was such a steady person in my life. But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Persistent Depressive Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 62 / credit_counselor_or_loan_officer / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.

There are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.

I guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. More like dark, discouraged thoughts where I don’t feel very attached to things. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to. I work with loans and credit counseling, so I’m used to paperwork and follow-up lists and details, but lately I’ll be sitting there staring at a loan worksheet or a client call list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd then I start putting things off, like making calls or deciding what to handle first, because even simple decisions feel harder than they should. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t always walk around crying or anything like that, so sometimes I almost talk myself out of calling it “sad,” but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to make me feel better — like going over to the community center, seeing people I know, or spending time in my garden — I’m there, and I’m doing the activity, but it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I’ll smile and chat because that’s what you do, you know, but it feels pasted on. Like I’m performing being okay more than actually feeling okay.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. At first I told myself it was just grief, you know, that of course I’d feel off and heavy for a while because she was such a steady person in my life. But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.\n\nThere are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. More like dark, discouraged thoughts where I don’t feel very attached to things. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are present most of the day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 10\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… most days, I think so. It’s not always the kind of sadness where I’m crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more like this dull heaviness that’s just sitting there with me, kind of in the background, and then sometimes I notice it’s been there for hours.

Even when I’m doing things that used to help — like going over to the community center, or being out in my garden — I can still do them, and I can smile and talk to people, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a pleasant expression on because that’s what you do, not because I actually feel lifted by it.

So yes, I’d say it’s there most of the day on most days. It may get a little quieter if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really feel gone.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to. I work with loans and credit counseling, so I’m used to paperwork and follow-up lists and details, but lately I’ll be sitting there staring at a loan worksheet or a client call list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd then I start putting things off, like making calls or deciding what to handle first, because even simple decisions feel harder than they should. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t always walk around crying or anything like that, so sometimes I almost talk myself out of calling it “sad,” but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to make me feel better — like going over to the community center, seeing people I know, or spending time in my garden — I’m there, and I’m doing the activity, but it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I’ll smile and chat because that’s what you do, you know, but it feels pasted on. Like I’m performing being okay more than actually feeling okay.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. At first I told myself it was just grief, you know, that of course I’d feel off and heavy for a while because she was such a steady person in my life. But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.\n\nThere are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. More like dark, discouraged thoughts where I don’t feel very attached to things. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think so. It’s not always the kind of sadness where I’m crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more like this dull heaviness that’s just sitting there with me, kind of in the background, and then sometimes I notice it’s been there for hours.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to help — like going over to the community center, or being out in my garden — I can still do them, and I can smile and talk to people, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a pleasant expression on because that’s what you do, not because I actually feel lifted by it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most of the day on most days. It may get a little quieter if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really feel gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think so. It’s not always the kind of sadness where I’m crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more like this dull heaviness that’s just sitting there with me, kind of in the background, and then sometimes I notice it’s been there for hours.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to help — like going over to the community center, or being out in my garden — I can still do them, and I can smile and talk to people, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a pleasant expression on because that’s what you do, not because I actually feel lifted by it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most of the day on most days. It may get a little quieter if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really feel gone.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities most days?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is, more than I probably wanted to admit at first.

At work, at the credit office, I’m still showing up and I’m still doing the basics, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups. And those are not really optional, you know? People are waiting to hear back about things, and I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” or “I’ll send that email first thing tomorrow,” and then it keeps sliding. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I’ve always tried to be very conscientious about that kind of thing. But it feels like it takes so much effort to pick up the phone and have the conversation, especially if I know it might be complicated or if someone is upset.

And outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, even small ones, and I had walking-club outings that I’d usually attend. Lately I’ve stepped back from those. Sometimes I’ll say I’m busy, but really I just don’t have the energy to get myself there and be pleasant and engaged. So yes, most days I can still function, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to. I work with loans and credit counseling, so I’m used to paperwork and follow-up lists and details, but lately I’ll be sitting there staring at a loan worksheet or a client call list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd then I start putting things off, like making calls or deciding what to handle first, because even simple decisions feel harder than they should. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t always walk around crying or anything like that, so sometimes I almost talk myself out of calling it “sad,” but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to make me feel better — like going over to the community center, seeing people I know, or spending time in my garden — I’m there, and I’m doing the activity, but it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I’ll smile and chat because that’s what you do, you know, but it feels pasted on. Like I’m performing being okay more than actually feeling okay.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. At first I told myself it was just grief, you know, that of course I’d feel off and heavy for a while because she was such a steady person in my life. But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.\n\nThere are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. More like dark, discouraged thoughts where I don’t feel very attached to things. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think so. It’s not always the kind of sadness where I’m crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more like this dull heaviness that’s just sitting there with me, kind of in the background, and then sometimes I notice it’s been there for hours.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to help — like going over to the community center, or being out in my garden — I can still do them, and I can smile and talk to people, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a pleasant expression on because that’s what you do, not because I actually feel lifted by it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most of the day on most days. It may get a little quieter if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really feel gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I probably wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work, at the credit office, I’m still showing up and I’m still doing the basics, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups. And those are not really optional, you know? People are waiting to hear back about things, and I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” or “I’ll send that email first thing tomorrow,” and then it keeps sliding. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I’ve always tried to be very conscientious about that kind of thing. But it feels like it takes so much effort to pick up the phone and have the conversation, especially if I know it might be complicated or if someone is upset.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, even small ones, and I had walking-club outings that I’d usually attend. Lately I’ve stepped back from those. Sometimes I’ll say I’m busy, but really I just don’t have the energy to get myself there and be pleasant and engaged. So yes, most days I can still function, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.\n\nThere are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. More like dark, discouraged thoughts where I don’t feel very attached to things. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think so. It’s not always the kind of sadness where I’m crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more like this dull heaviness that’s just sitting there with me, kind of in the background, and then sometimes I notice it’s been there for hours.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to help — like going over to the community center, or being out in my garden — I can still do them, and I can smile and talk to people, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a pleasant expression on because that’s what you do, not because I actually feel lifted by it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most of the day on most days. It may get a little quieter if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really feel gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I probably wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work, at the credit office, I’m still showing up and I’m still doing the basics, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups. And those are not really optional, you know? People are waiting to hear back about things, and I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” or “I’ll send that email first thing tomorrow,” and then it keeps sliding. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I’ve always tried to be very conscientious about that kind of thing. But it feels like it takes so much effort to pick up the phone and have the conversation, especially if I know it might be complicated or if someone is upset.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, even small ones, and I had walking-club outings that I’d usually attend. Lately I’ve stepped back from those. Sometimes I’ll say I’m busy, but really I just don’t have the energy to get myself there and be pleasant and engaged. So yes, most days I can still function, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think “nearly always” is fair, though I almost hesitate to say it because it sounds so dramatic. It’s not like I’m actively sobbing all day or completely unable to speak to people. I can still go through my day, and if someone sees me at the community center, they might think I’m doing all right because I’ll smile, I’ll make conversation, I’ll ask how they’re doing.

But inside, most days, there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me. It’s like I carry it from place to place. Even in my garden, which used to really settle me and give me some peace, I find myself going through the motions — watering, trimming, fussing with things — but not feeling the same lift from it. Same with being around people. I can put the pleasant face on, but it feels pasted on a lot of the time.

So yes, I’d say it’s present most of the time. It may fade into the background here and there, but it doesn’t really leave.

It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t always walk around crying or anything like that, so sometimes I almost talk myself out of calling it “sad,” but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to make me feel better — like going over to the community center, seeing people I know, or spending time in my garden — I’m there, and I’m doing the activity, but it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.\n\nThere are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while.
