But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.\n\nThere are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. More like dark, discouraged thoughts where I don’t feel very attached to things. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think so. It’s not always the kind of sadness where I’m crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more like this dull heaviness that’s just sitting there with me, kind of in the background, and then sometimes I notice it’s been there for hours.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to help — like going over to the community center, or being out in my garden — I can still do them, and I can smile and talk to people, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a pleasant expression on because that’s what you do, not because I actually feel lifted by it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most of the day on most days. It may get a little quieter if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really feel gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I probably wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work, at the credit office, I’m still showing up and I’m still doing the basics, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups. And those are not really optional, you know? People are waiting to hear back about things, and I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” or “I’ll send that email first thing tomorrow,” and then it keeps sliding. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I’ve always tried to be very conscientious about that kind of thing. But it feels like it takes so much effort to pick up the phone and have the conversation, especially if I know it might be complicated or if someone is upset.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, even small ones, and I had walking-club outings that I’d usually attend. Lately I’ve stepped back from those. Sometimes I’ll say I’m busy, but really I just don’t have the energy to get myself there and be pleasant and engaged. So yes, most days I can still function, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are severe enough to nearly always be present?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “nearly always” is fair, though I almost hesitate to say it because it sounds so dramatic. It’s not like I’m actively sobbing all day or completely unable to speak to people. I can still go through my day, and if someone sees me at the community center, they might think I’m doing all right because I’ll smile, I’ll make conversation, I’ll ask how they’re doing.\n\nBut inside, most days, there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me. It’s like I carry it from place to place. Even in my garden, which used to really settle me and give me some peace, I find myself going through the motions — watering, trimming, fussing with things — but not feeling the same lift from it. Same with being around people. I can put the pleasant face on, but it feels pasted on a lot of the time.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s present most of the time. It may fade into the background here and there, but it doesn’t really leave.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find no pleasure in at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no pleasure at all” in every single thing, but… yes, there are things that used to really lift me up that just don’t feel the same anymore.\n\nThe community center, for example. I used to look forward to going there — seeing familiar faces, chatting a little, feeling like I was part of something. And I still go sometimes, and I can still smile and have a perfectly normal conversation, but inside it feels very flat. Like I’m doing the motions of being sociable, but I’m not really getting that warm feeling from it.\n\nAnd my garden is probably the one that bothers me most. That used to be such a comfort to me. I could lose track of time out there, fussing over the plants, noticing little changes, feeling peaceful. Now I’ll go out and water or trim things, but there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me most days, even there. I’ll think, “This should feel good,” and instead it’s more like I’m just completing a task.\n\nSo yes… those places still matter to me, I suppose, but the pleasure feels very muted. Sometimes it’s like I’ve got a smile pasted on, even doing things I used to genuinely enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most of the time, yes. I still do things, so it isn’t that I’ve completely stopped everything, but the interest feels much more like something I’m forcing than something I naturally feel.\n\nLike with the community center, I’ll still go sometimes, and I can sit there and talk and smile and seem normal enough, but inside I’m not really enjoying it the way I used to. It feels like I’m participating from behind a pane of glass or something. And in my garden, which used to be one of the main things that helped me feel calm and happy, I find myself just watering or trimming because it needs doing, not because I’m drawn to it.\n\nMost days there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me, and it follows me even into places that used to lift me up. So I wouldn’t say I have no interest in anything at all, but I do feel like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time, with a smile kind of pasted on.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are significantly impacting your ability to make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. That’s actually part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to, and then even small decisions start feeling much bigger than they should.\n\nAt work, I’ll be looking at a loan worksheet or a client follow-up list — things I’ve handled for years — and I’ll realize I’ve reread the same line three or four times and I still haven’t really taken it in. Then I sit there thinking, “Okay, just make the call, just decide what needs to be done next,” and somehow I put it off. Not because it’s complicated, necessarily, but because my mind feels slow and heavy, like I can’t quite sort through it.\n\nEven simple choices feel harder. I’ll spend too long deciding which client to call first, or whether I should finish one form before starting another, and then I get frustrated with myself because these are not decisions that used to trip me up. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I know what I’m supposed to do, but getting my mind to settle and choose feels like pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you are unable to derive any pleasure from at all?\n\nPatient: I think I may have already touched on this, but… yes, those are the two that come to mind most clearly — the community center and my garden.\n\nI don’t know if I can honestly say I feel absolutely nothing every single time, but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sits with me. Even when I’m somewhere that used to lift me up, like the community center, I feel like I’m sort of performing. I’ll smile, I’ll chat, I’ll ask people how they are, and from the outside I probably look fine. But inside it feels flat, like I’m not really getting anything back from it.\n\nAnd with the garden… that one is hard, because it used to feel peaceful to me. I used to notice the small things and feel soothed by it. Now I’ll be out there watering or trimming, and it’s like I’m just checking something off a list. I keep thinking I should be enjoying it, but mostly I just feel like I’m going through the motions with a smile pasted on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. I don’t mean it was like a light switch that same day, exactly, but when I look back, that’s really when everything started to lose its color.

At first I thought it was just grief, and maybe some of it was. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course you don’t feel like going out, of course the garden doesn’t feel the same right now.” But then months kept passing, and it didn’t really lift the way I expected it to. The things I used to look forward to just stayed kind of flat.

So yes, I’d say since she died — a little over two years ago — that’s how long I’ve been feeling this loss of interest most of the time.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to. I work with loans and credit counseling, so I’m used to paperwork and follow-up lists and details, but lately I’ll be sitting there staring at a loan worksheet or a client call list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd then I start putting things off, like making calls or deciding what to handle first, because even simple decisions feel harder than they should. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t always walk around crying or anything like that, so sometimes I almost talk myself out of calling it “sad,” but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to make me feel better — like going over to the community center, seeing people I know, or spending time in my garden — I’m there, and I’m doing the activity, but it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I’ll smile and chat because that’s what you do, you know, but it feels pasted on. Like I’m performing being okay more than actually feeling okay.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. At first I told myself it was just grief, you know, that of course I’d feel off and heavy for a while because she was such a steady person in my life. But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.\n\nThere are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. More like dark, discouraged thoughts where I don’t feel very attached to things. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think so. It’s not always the kind of sadness where I’m crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more like this dull heaviness that’s just sitting there with me, kind of in the background, and then sometimes I notice it’s been there for hours.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to help — like going over to the community center, or being out in my garden — I can still do them, and I can smile and talk to people, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a pleasant expression on because that’s what you do, not because I actually feel lifted by it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most of the day on most days. It may get a little quieter if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really feel gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I probably wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work, at the credit office, I’m still showing up and I’m still doing the basics, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups. And those are not really optional, you know? People are waiting to hear back about things, and I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” or “I’ll send that email first thing tomorrow,” and then it keeps sliding. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I’ve always tried to be very conscientious about that kind of thing. But it feels like it takes so much effort to pick up the phone and have the conversation, especially if I know it might be complicated or if someone is upset.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, even small ones, and I had walking-club outings that I’d usually attend. Lately I’ve stepped back from those. Sometimes I’ll say I’m busy, but really I just don’t have the energy to get myself there and be pleasant and engaged. So yes, most days I can still function, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are severe enough to nearly always be present?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “nearly always” is fair, though I almost hesitate to say it because it sounds so dramatic. It’s not like I’m actively sobbing all day or completely unable to speak to people. I can still go through my day, and if someone sees me at the community center, they might think I’m doing all right because I’ll smile, I’ll make conversation, I’ll ask how they’re doing.\n\nBut inside, most days, there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me. It’s like I carry it from place to place. Even in my garden, which used to really settle me and give me some peace, I find myself going through the motions — watering, trimming, fussing with things — but not feeling the same lift from it. Same with being around people. I can put the pleasant face on, but it feels pasted on a lot of the time.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s present most of the time. It may fade into the background here and there, but it doesn’t really leave.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find no pleasure in at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no pleasure at all” in every single thing, but… yes, there are things that used to really lift me up that just don’t feel the same anymore.\n\nThe community center, for example. I used to look forward to going there — seeing familiar faces, chatting a little, feeling like I was part of something. And I still go sometimes, and I can still smile and have a perfectly normal conversation, but inside it feels very flat. Like I’m doing the motions of being sociable, but I’m not really getting that warm feeling from it.\n\nAnd my garden is probably the one that bothers me most. That used to be such a comfort to me. I could lose track of time out there, fussing over the plants, noticing little changes, feeling peaceful. Now I’ll go out and water or trim things, but there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me most days, even there. I’ll think, “This should feel good,” and instead it’s more like I’m just completing a task.\n\nSo yes… those places still matter to me, I suppose, but the pleasure feels very muted. Sometimes it’s like I’ve got a smile pasted on, even doing things I used to genuinely enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most of the time, yes. I still do things, so it isn’t that I’ve completely stopped everything, but the interest feels much more like something I’m forcing than something I naturally feel.\n\nLike with the community center, I’ll still go sometimes, and I can sit there and talk and smile and seem normal enough, but inside I’m not really enjoying it the way I used to. It feels like I’m participating from behind a pane of glass or something. And in my garden, which used to be one of the main things that helped me feel calm and happy, I find myself just watering or trimming because it needs doing, not because I’m drawn to it.\n\nMost days there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me, and it follows me even into places that used to lift me up. So I wouldn’t say I have no interest in anything at all, but I do feel like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time, with a smile kind of pasted on.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are significantly impacting your ability to make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. That’s actually part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to, and then even small decisions start feeling much bigger than they should.\n\nAt work, I’ll be looking at a loan worksheet or a client follow-up list — things I’ve handled for years — and I’ll realize I’ve reread the same line three or four times and I still haven’t really taken it in. Then I sit there thinking, “Okay, just make the call, just decide what needs to be done next,” and somehow I put it off. Not because it’s complicated, necessarily, but because my mind feels slow and heavy, like I can’t quite sort through it.\n\nEven simple choices feel harder. I’ll spend too long deciding which client to call first, or whether I should finish one form before starting another, and then I get frustrated with myself because these are not decisions that used to trip me up. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I know what I’m supposed to do, but getting my mind to settle and choose feels like pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you are unable to derive any pleasure from at all?\n\nPatient: I think I may have already touched on this, but… yes, those are the two that come to mind most clearly — the community center and my garden.\n\nI don’t know if I can honestly say I feel absolutely nothing every single time, but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sits with me. Even when I’m somewhere that used to lift me up, like the community center, I feel like I’m sort of performing. I’ll smile, I’ll chat, I’ll ask people how they are, and from the outside I probably look fine. But inside it feels flat, like I’m not really getting anything back from it.\n\nAnd with the garden… that one is hard, because it used to feel peaceful to me. I used to notice the small things and feel soothed by it. Now I’ll be out there watering or trimming, and it’s like I’m just checking something off a list. I keep thinking I should be enjoying it, but mostly I just feel like I’m going through the motions with a smile pasted on.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you felt like you've lost interest in most activities?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. I don’t mean it was like a light switch that same day, exactly, but when I look back, that’s really when everything started to lose its color.\n\nAt first I thought it was just grief, and maybe some of it was. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course you don’t feel like going out, of course the garden doesn’t feel the same right now.” But then months kept passing, and it didn’t really lift the way I expected it to. The things I used to look forward to just stayed kind of flat.\n\nSo yes, I’d say since she died — a little over two years ago — that’s how long I’ve been feeling this loss of interest most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… nearly every day, I would say. It’s not always that I’m crying or anything dramatic like that, but most days there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me. It’s just there when I wake up, and then it kind of follows me through the day.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to. I work with loans and credit counseling, so I’m used to paperwork and follow-up lists and details, but lately I’ll be sitting there staring at a loan worksheet or a client call list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd then I start putting things off, like making calls or deciding what to handle first, because even simple decisions feel harder than they should. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t always walk around crying or anything like that, so sometimes I almost talk myself out of calling it “sad,” but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to make me feel better — like going over to the community center, seeing people I know, or spending time in my garden — I’m there, and I’m doing the activity, but it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I’ll smile and chat because that’s what you do, you know, but it feels pasted on. Like I’m performing being okay more than actually feeling okay.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. At first I told myself it was just grief, you know, that of course I’d feel off and heavy for a while because she was such a steady person in my life. But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.\n\nThere are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. More like dark, discouraged thoughts where I don’t feel very attached to things. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think so. It’s not always the kind of sadness where I’m crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more like this dull heaviness that’s just sitting there with me, kind of in the background, and then sometimes I notice it’s been there for hours.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to help — like going over to the community center, or being out in my garden — I can still do them, and I can smile and talk to people, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a pleasant expression on because that’s what you do, not because I actually feel lifted by it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most of the day on most days. It may get a little quieter if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really feel gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I probably wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work, at the credit office, I’m still showing up and I’m still doing the basics, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups. And those are not really optional, you know? People are waiting to hear back about things, and I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” or “I’ll send that email first thing tomorrow,” and then it keeps sliding. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I’ve always tried to be very conscientious about that kind of thing. But it feels like it takes so much effort to pick up the phone and have the conversation, especially if I know it might be complicated or if someone is upset.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, even small ones, and I had walking-club outings that I’d usually attend. Lately I’ve stepped back from those. Sometimes I’ll say I’m busy, but really I just don’t have the energy to get myself there and be pleasant and engaged. So yes, most days I can still function, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are severe enough to nearly always be present?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “nearly always” is fair, though I almost hesitate to say it because it sounds so dramatic. It’s not like I’m actively sobbing all day or completely unable to speak to people. I can still go through my day, and if someone sees me at the community center, they might think I’m doing all right because I’ll smile, I’ll make conversation, I’ll ask how they’re doing.\n\nBut inside, most days, there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me. It’s like I carry it from place to place. Even in my garden, which used to really settle me and give me some peace, I find myself going through the motions — watering, trimming, fussing with things — but not feeling the same lift from it. Same with being around people. I can put the pleasant face on, but it feels pasted on a lot of the time.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s present most of the time. It may fade into the background here and there, but it doesn’t really leave.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find no pleasure in at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no pleasure at all” in every single thing, but… yes, there are things that used to really lift me up that just don’t feel the same anymore.\n\nThe community center, for example. I used to look forward to going there — seeing familiar faces, chatting a little, feeling like I was part of something. And I still go sometimes, and I can still smile and have a perfectly normal conversation, but inside it feels very flat. Like I’m doing the motions of being sociable, but I’m not really getting that warm feeling from it.\n\nAnd my garden is probably the one that bothers me most. That used to be such a comfort to me. I could lose track of time out there, fussing over the plants, noticing little changes, feeling peaceful. Now I’ll go out and water or trim things, but there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me most days, even there. I’ll think, “This should feel good,” and instead it’s more like I’m just completing a task.\n\nSo yes… those places still matter to me, I suppose, but the pleasure feels very muted. Sometimes it’s like I’ve got a smile pasted on, even doing things I used to genuinely enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most of the time, yes. I still do things, so it isn’t that I’ve completely stopped everything, but the interest feels much more like something I’m forcing than something I naturally feel.\n\nLike with the community center, I’ll still go sometimes, and I can sit there and talk and smile and seem normal enough, but inside I’m not really enjoying it the way I used to. It feels like I’m participating from behind a pane of glass or something. And in my garden, which used to be one of the main things that helped me feel calm and happy, I find myself just watering or trimming because it needs doing, not because I’m drawn to it.\n\nMost days there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me, and it follows me even into places that used to lift me up. So I wouldn’t say I have no interest in anything at all, but I do feel like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time, with a smile kind of pasted on.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are significantly impacting your ability to make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. That’s actually part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to, and then even small decisions start feeling much bigger than they should.\n\nAt work, I’ll be looking at a loan worksheet or a client follow-up list — things I’ve handled for years — and I’ll realize I’ve reread the same line three or four times and I still haven’t really taken it in. Then I sit there thinking, “Okay, just make the call, just decide what needs to be done next,” and somehow I put it off. Not because it’s complicated, necessarily, but because my mind feels slow and heavy, like I can’t quite sort through it.\n\nEven simple choices feel harder. I’ll spend too long deciding which client to call first, or whether I should finish one form before starting another, and then I get frustrated with myself because these are not decisions that used to trip me up. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I know what I’m supposed to do, but getting my mind to settle and choose feels like pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you are unable to derive any pleasure from at all?\n\nPatient: I think I may have already touched on this, but… yes, those are the two that come to mind most clearly — the community center and my garden.\n\nI don’t know if I can honestly say I feel absolutely nothing every single time, but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sits with me. Even when I’m somewhere that used to lift me up, like the community center, I feel like I’m sort of performing. I’ll smile, I’ll chat, I’ll ask people how they are, and from the outside I probably look fine. But inside it feels flat, like I’m not really getting anything back from it.\n\nAnd with the garden… that one is hard, because it used to feel peaceful to me. I used to notice the small things and feel soothed by it. Now I’ll be out there watering or trimming, and it’s like I’m just checking something off a list. I keep thinking I should be enjoying it, but mostly I just feel like I’m going through the motions with a smile pasted on.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you felt like you've lost interest in most activities?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. I don’t mean it was like a light switch that same day, exactly, but when I look back, that’s really when everything started to lose its color.\n\nAt first I thought it was just grief, and maybe some of it was. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course you don’t feel like going out, of course the garden doesn’t feel the same right now.” But then months kept passing, and it didn’t really lift the way I expected it to. The things I used to look forward to just stayed kind of flat.\n\nSo yes, I’d say since she died — a little over two years ago — that’s how long I’ve been feeling this loss of interest most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I would say. It’s not always that I’m crying or anything dramatic like that, but most days there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it nearly overwhelming most of the time. It’s more like… a constant weight, if that makes sense. Most days there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me, and it doesn’t really go away just because I’m doing something familiar or something that used to make me feel better.

Like at the community center, I can still show up, I can still talk to people and smile and do the pleasant small-talk kind of thing. And in the garden, I can still water things or pull weeds or whatever needs doing. But inside, it feels like I’m just going through the motions with a smile pasted on. It’s heavy, and it’s there nearly every day, but not always in a way where I feel like I’m about to collapse under it. More like it wears me down quietly.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I came in because I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to. I work with loans and credit counseling, so I’m used to paperwork and follow-up lists and details, but lately I’ll be sitting there staring at a loan worksheet or a client call list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd then I start putting things off, like making calls or deciding what to handle first, because even simple decisions feel harder than they should. It’s frustrating, because I know how to do my job, I’ve done it for years, but my mind just feels… not sharp, I guess. Like I’m trying to push through fog. That’s what finally made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t always walk around crying or anything like that, so sometimes I almost talk myself out of calling it “sad,” but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to make me feel better — like going over to the community center, seeing people I know, or spending time in my garden — I’m there, and I’m doing the activity, but it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I’ll smile and chat because that’s what you do, you know, but it feels pasted on. Like I’m performing being okay more than actually feeling okay.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. At first I told myself it was just grief, you know, that of course I’d feel off and heavy for a while because she was such a steady person in my life. But then months kept going by, and then a year, and now it’s been more than two years, and I still don’t feel like I really came back to how I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that, to be honest. I mean, I know people ask about sleep and appetite with this sort of thing, but I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way.\n\nSome days I eat normally, and other days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about lunch until late, but I don’t know that I’d call it a major appetite change. And sleep… I don’t know. I’ve never been one of those people who sleeps perfectly every night anyway, so it’s hard for me to say what’s new and what’s just me getting older or having things on my mind.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: nothing dramatic that I can point to. I’d probably have to pay closer attention to tell you for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of sadness and heaviness is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life in a more significant way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than mild at this point. I still get up and do what I have to do, so from the outside maybe it looks like I’m managing, but it’s definitely interfering.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started postponing client follow-ups at the credit office. And that bothers me, because I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money or loans or whatever situation they’re in. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch comes and I push it to the next morning, and then I’m sitting there feeling guilty about it. It’s not that I don’t care. It’s like everything takes more effort to start.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, and I was pretty regular with the walking club outings. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.\n\nThere are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. More like dark, discouraged thoughts where I don’t feel very attached to things. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think so. It’s not always the kind of sadness where I’m crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more like this dull heaviness that’s just sitting there with me, kind of in the background, and then sometimes I notice it’s been there for hours.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to help — like going over to the community center, or being out in my garden — I can still do them, and I can smile and talk to people, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a pleasant expression on because that’s what you do, not because I actually feel lifted by it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most of the day on most days. It may get a little quieter if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really feel gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I probably wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work, at the credit office, I’m still showing up and I’m still doing the basics, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups. And those are not really optional, you know? People are waiting to hear back about things, and I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” or “I’ll send that email first thing tomorrow,” and then it keeps sliding. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I’ve always tried to be very conscientious about that kind of thing. But it feels like it takes so much effort to pick up the phone and have the conversation, especially if I know it might be complicated or if someone is upset.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, even small ones, and I had walking-club outings that I’d usually attend. Lately I’ve stepped back from those. Sometimes I’ll say I’m busy, but really I just don’t have the energy to get myself there and be pleasant and engaged. So yes, most days I can still function, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are severe enough to nearly always be present?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “nearly always” is fair, though I almost hesitate to say it because it sounds so dramatic. It’s not like I’m actively sobbing all day or completely unable to speak to people. I can still go through my day, and if someone sees me at the community center, they might think I’m doing all right because I’ll smile, I’ll make conversation, I’ll ask how they’re doing.\n\nBut inside, most days, there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me. It’s like I carry it from place to place. Even in my garden, which used to really settle me and give me some peace, I find myself going through the motions — watering, trimming, fussing with things — but not feeling the same lift from it. Same with being around people. I can put the pleasant face on, but it feels pasted on a lot of the time.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s present most of the time. It may fade into the background here and there, but it doesn’t really leave.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find no pleasure in at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no pleasure at all” in every single thing, but… yes, there are things that used to really lift me up that just don’t feel the same anymore.\n\nThe community center, for example. I used to look forward to going there — seeing familiar faces, chatting a little, feeling like I was part of something. And I still go sometimes, and I can still smile and have a perfectly normal conversation, but inside it feels very flat. Like I’m doing the motions of being sociable, but I’m not really getting that warm feeling from it.\n\nAnd my garden is probably the one that bothers me most. That used to be such a comfort to me. I could lose track of time out there, fussing over the plants, noticing little changes, feeling peaceful. Now I’ll go out and water or trim things, but there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me most days, even there. I’ll think, “This should feel good,” and instead it’s more like I’m just completing a task.\n\nSo yes… those places still matter to me, I suppose, but the pleasure feels very muted. Sometimes it’s like I’ve got a smile pasted on, even doing things I used to genuinely enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most of the time, yes. I still do things, so it isn’t that I’ve completely stopped everything, but the interest feels much more like something I’m forcing than something I naturally feel.\n\nLike with the community center, I’ll still go sometimes, and I can sit there and talk and smile and seem normal enough, but inside I’m not really enjoying it the way I used to. It feels like I’m participating from behind a pane of glass or something. And in my garden, which used to be one of the main things that helped me feel calm and happy, I find myself just watering or trimming because it needs doing, not because I’m drawn to it.\n\nMost days there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me, and it follows me even into places that used to lift me up. So I wouldn’t say I have no interest in anything at all, but I do feel like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time, with a smile kind of pasted on.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are significantly impacting your ability to make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. That’s actually part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to, and then even small decisions start feeling much bigger than they should.\n\nAt work, I’ll be looking at a loan worksheet or a client follow-up list — things I’ve handled for years — and I’ll realize I’ve reread the same line three or four times and I still haven’t really taken it in. Then I sit there thinking, “Okay, just make the call, just decide what needs to be done next,” and somehow I put it off. Not because it’s complicated, necessarily, but because my mind feels slow and heavy, like I can’t quite sort through it.\n\nEven simple choices feel harder. I’ll spend too long deciding which client to call first, or whether I should finish one form before starting another, and then I get frustrated with myself because these are not decisions that used to trip me up. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I know what I’m supposed to do, but getting my mind to settle and choose feels like pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you are unable to derive any pleasure from at all?\n\nPatient: I think I may have already touched on this, but… yes, those are the two that come to mind most clearly — the community center and my garden.\n\nI don’t know if I can honestly say I feel absolutely nothing every single time, but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sits with me. Even when I’m somewhere that used to lift me up, like the community center, I feel like I’m sort of performing. I’ll smile, I’ll chat, I’ll ask people how they are, and from the outside I probably look fine. But inside it feels flat, like I’m not really getting anything back from it.\n\nAnd with the garden… that one is hard, because it used to feel peaceful to me. I used to notice the small things and feel soothed by it. Now I’ll be out there watering or trimming, and it’s like I’m just checking something off a list. I keep thinking I should be enjoying it, but mostly I just feel like I’m going through the motions with a smile pasted on.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you felt like you've lost interest in most activities?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course you don’t feel like going out, of course the garden doesn’t feel the same right now.” But then months kept passing, and it didn’t really lift the way I expected it to. Those were things that helped me feel connected, like I was still part of the world a little bit. But lately I’ll see something on the calendar and just… not go. Or I’ll decide it’s too much trouble, even though it isn’t really. Then afterward I feel worse because I know I’m isolating myself. So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that in a clean yes-or-no way. I mean, I still know, in my head, that certain things are nice or worthwhile, if that makes sense. Like if someone says there’s an exhibit opening or a group walk happening, I can recognize that those are things I used to look forward to.\n\nBut whether I actually feel the enjoyment the same way… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should go, I’ll probably feel better once I’m there,” and then I just don’t have much pull toward it. And if I do go, it’s not always unpleasant, exactly. It’s more like I’m watching myself participate rather than really being in it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I never enjoy anything. That sounds too absolute. But I do think the pleasure is… muted, maybe. Less reliable. Things that used to feel easy and satisfying now feel like they take effort before I can even get to the part where I might enjoy them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one thing and say, “Yes, that still gives me real pleasure,” in the way it used to. There are little moments, I suppose. Like sometimes if I’m on a walk and the weather is just right, or I see something pretty in a shop window or at a gallery, I can still appreciate it. I know it’s nice. I’m not numb to everything.\n\nBut it’s more fleeting than it used to be. It doesn’t seem to last very long, and it takes more effort to get myself into the situation where I might enjoy it in the first place. Sometimes the idea of doing something feels heavier than the activity itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there are small bits of pleasure here and there, but I wouldn’t say there’s anything I’m consistently looking forward to right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're at a point where you think life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. I don’t think I sit there with a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I get tired of feeling this way, and I can have these stretches where everything feels flat and heavy and I think, “Is this just how it’s going to be?” But that’s not quite the same as wanting my life to be over.\n\nI guess I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to brush it off. There are moments where I feel worn down, like I don’t really see the point of much in that moment. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve reached a point where I truly believe life isn’t worth living. It’s more like I’m discouraged and exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly. I don’t think I’ve had a plan or anything like that, if that’s what you’re asking. The word “harming myself” feels very serious, and I don’t want to say yes if what I mean is more… I get overwhelmed, or I feel worn down, or I don’t care about things the way I should.\n\nThere are times where I feel so tired of myself, or tired of how heavy everything feels, that I just want it to stop for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as sitting there thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself.” At least, not in a clear way.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really had specific thoughts of harming myself. More like dark, discouraged thoughts where I don’t feel very attached to things. But not a plan, and not something I’ve been preparing to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I think so. It’s not always the kind of sadness where I’m crying or anything obvious like that. It’s more like this dull heaviness that’s just sitting there with me, kind of in the background, and then sometimes I notice it’s been there for hours.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to help — like going over to the community center, or being out in my garden — I can still do them, and I can smile and talk to people, but a lot of the time it feels like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a pleasant expression on because that’s what you do, not because I actually feel lifted by it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s there most of the day on most days. It may get a little quieter if I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really feel gone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these feelings of sadness and heaviness are interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I probably wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work, at the credit office, I’m still showing up and I’m still doing the basics, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups. And those are not really optional, you know? People are waiting to hear back about things, and I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” or “I’ll send that email first thing tomorrow,” and then it keeps sliding. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I’ve always tried to be very conscientious about that kind of thing. But it feels like it takes so much effort to pick up the phone and have the conversation, especially if I know it might be complicated or if someone is upset.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a point of going to gallery events, even small ones, and I had walking-club outings that I’d usually attend. Lately I’ve stepped back from those. Sometimes I’ll say I’m busy, but really I just don’t have the energy to get myself there and be pleasant and engaged. So yes, most days I can still function, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are severe enough to nearly always be present?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “nearly always” is fair, though I almost hesitate to say it because it sounds so dramatic. It’s not like I’m actively sobbing all day or completely unable to speak to people. I can still go through my day, and if someone sees me at the community center, they might think I’m doing all right because I’ll smile, I’ll make conversation, I’ll ask how they’re doing.\n\nBut inside, most days, there’s this dull heaviness that just sort of sits with me. It’s like I carry it from place to place. Even in my garden, which used to really settle me and give me some peace, I find myself going through the motions — watering, trimming, fussing with things — but not feeling the same lift from it. Same with being around people. I can put the pleasant face on, but it feels pasted on a lot of the time.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s present most of the time. It may fade into the background here and there, but it doesn’t really leave.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find no pleasure in at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no pleasure at all” in every single thing, but… yes, there are things that used to really lift me up that just don’t feel the same anymore.\n\nThe community center, for example. I used to look forward to going there — seeing familiar faces, chatting a little, feeling like I was part of something. And I still go sometimes, and I can still smile and have a perfectly normal conversation, but inside it feels very flat. Like I’m doing the motions of being sociable, but I’m not really getting that warm feeling from it.\n\nAnd my garden is probably the one that bothers me most. That used to be such a comfort to me. I could lose track of time out there, fussing over the plants, noticing little changes, feeling peaceful. Now I’ll go out and water or trim things, but there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me most days, even there. I’ll think, “This should feel good,” and instead it’s more like I’m just completing a task.\n\nSo yes… those places still matter to me, I suppose, but the pleasure feels very muted. Sometimes it’s like I’ve got a smile pasted on, even doing things I used to genuinely enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think most of the time, yes. I still do things, so it isn’t that I’ve completely stopped everything, but the interest feels much more like something I’m forcing than something I naturally feel.\n\nLike with the community center, I’ll still go sometimes, and I can sit there and talk and smile and seem normal enough, but inside I’m not really enjoying it the way I used to. It feels like I’m participating from behind a pane of glass or something. And in my garden, which used to be one of the main things that helped me feel calm and happy, I find myself just watering or trimming because it needs doing, not because I’m drawn to it.\n\nMost days there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me, and it follows me even into places that used to lift me up. So I wouldn’t say I have no interest in anything at all, but I do feel like I’m going through the motions a lot of the time, with a smile kind of pasted on.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are significantly impacting your ability to make decisions?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. That’s actually part of why I came in, because I can’t seem to keep my mind on things the way I used to, and then even small decisions start feeling much bigger than they should.\n\nAt work, I’ll be looking at a loan worksheet or a client follow-up list — things I’ve handled for years — and I’ll realize I’ve reread the same line three or four times and I still haven’t really taken it in. Then I sit there thinking, “Okay, just make the call, just decide what needs to be done next,” and somehow I put it off. Not because it’s complicated, necessarily, but because my mind feels slow and heavy, like I can’t quite sort through it.\n\nEven simple choices feel harder. I’ll spend too long deciding which client to call first, or whether I should finish one form before starting another, and then I get frustrated with myself because these are not decisions that used to trip me up. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I know what I’m supposed to do, but getting my mind to settle and choose feels like pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you are unable to derive any pleasure from at all?\n\nPatient: I think I may have already touched on this, but… yes, those are the two that come to mind most clearly — the community center and my garden.\n\nI don’t know if I can honestly say I feel absolutely nothing every single time, but most days there’s this dull heaviness that just sits with me. Even when I’m somewhere that used to lift me up, like the community center, I feel like I’m sort of performing. I’ll smile, I’ll chat, I’ll ask people how they are, and from the outside I probably look fine. But inside it feels flat, like I’m not really getting anything back from it.\n\nAnd with the garden… that one is hard, because it used to feel peaceful to me. I used to notice the small things and feel soothed by it. Now I’ll be out there watering or trimming, and it’s like I’m just checking something off a list. I keep thinking I should be enjoying it, but mostly I just feel like I’m going through the motions with a smile pasted on.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you felt like you've lost interest in most activities?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. It started after my close friend died. I don’t mean it was like a light switch that same day, exactly, but when I look back, that’s really when everything started to lose its color.\n\nAt first I thought it was just grief, and maybe some of it was. I kept telling myself, “Well, of course you don’t feel like going out, of course the garden doesn’t feel the same right now.” But then months kept passing, and it didn’t really lift the way I expected it to. The things I used to look forward to just stayed kind of flat.\n\nSo yes, I’d say since she died — a little over two years ago — that’s how long I’ve been feeling this loss of interest most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings of sadness and heaviness are present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I would say. It’s not always that I’m crying or anything dramatic like that, but most days there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me. It’s just there when I wake up, and then it kind of follows me through the day.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that used to help — like going to the community center or being out in my garden — I still feel it. I can be talking to people, smiling, doing what I’m supposed to do, and from the outside I probably seem okay. But inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions with a smile pasted on. The heaviness doesn’t really leave just because I’m somewhere that used to lift me up.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your current feelings of sadness and heaviness as intense, to the point where they are nearly overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it nearly overwhelming most of the time. It’s more like… a constant weight, if that makes sense. Yes… I think it is, more than I probably like to admit. I’m still getting through the basics, so from the outside it may not look like I’ve fallen apart, but I can see the ways it’s changed how I function.

At work, at the credit office, I’ve started putting off client follow-ups. Not all of them, but enough that I notice it. I’ll have a call I know I need to make, or paperwork tied to someone’s account, and I’ll tell myself, “I’ll do it after lunch,” or “first thing tomorrow,” and then it sits there longer than it should. That’s not really like me — I’ve always tried to be dependable with people, especially when they’re already stressed about money.

And outside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to make a real point of going to gallery events, even small local ones, and I had a walking club I’d go out with pretty regularly. Lately I just… don’t go, or I say maybe and then don’t follow through. It feels like too much effort to get myself there, and then I feel bad afterward because those were things that used to keep me connected.

So yes, I’d say it’s affecting my daily life. I’m still functioning, but not the way I used to.
